Global-in-time Well-posedness of Classical Solutions to the Vacuum Free Boundary Problem for the 1-D Viscous Saint-Venant System with Large Data
Zhouping Xin, Jiawen Zhang, Shengguo Zhu

Abstract
. This paper establishes the global existence and uniqueness of classical solutions to the vacuum free boundary problem for the 1-D viscous Saint-Venant system for laminar shallow water with a general class of large initial data. Since the fluid depth ρ \rho vanishes on the moving boundary, the momentum equations degenerate in both time evolution and spatial dissipation, potentially causing singularities in velocity derivatives and making classical solutions difficult to construct. By exploiting the intrinsic degenerate-singular structures, we identify admissible initial depth profiles for which ρ 0 α \rho_{0}^{\alpha} belongs to H 3 H^{3} and vanishes as the distance to the moving boundary, with 1 3 < α ≤ 1 \frac{1}{3}<\alpha\leq 1 . For 1 3 < α < 1 \frac{1}{3}<\alpha<1 , ρ 0 \rho_{0} satisfies the BD entropy condition; for α = 1 \alpha=1 , it satisfies the physical vacuum boundary condition but violates the BD entropy condition. The solutions are classical up to the moving boundary. First, we introduce new weighted nonlinear energy functionals involving lower- and higher-order derivatives, based on the balance between pressure and spatial dissipation. A new cross-derivatives embedding theorem yields the required elliptic estimates and, together with a modified Galerkin scheme, establishes local existence and uniqueness. Second, we derive new global weighted L p L^{p} estimates, 2 ≤ p ≤ ∞ 2\leq p\leq\infty , for the effective velocity v = u + ( log ⁡ ρ ) y v=u+(\log\rho)_{y} , where y y is the Eulerian coordinate, using the transport properties of its evolution equation. These estimates differ from those of Bresch-Desjardins (Comm. Math. Phys. 238 (2003), 211-223) and Kanel (Differ. Uravn. 4 (1968), 721-734) and are crucial for α = 1 \alpha=1 , where the BD entropy condition fails. Finally, we establish weighted energy estimates for first-order velocity derivatives with weights involving powers of ρ 0 \rho_{0} and η x \eta_{x} , where x x is the Lagrangian coordinate and η \eta the flow map. These estimates give an upper bound for η x \eta_{x} on every finite time interval without any smallness assumption on the initial data. Further singular or degenerate weighted energy estimates yield the desired global regularity.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35A01, 35A09, 35B65, 35Q30, 76N06, 76N10.

1.
Introduction

The time evolution of a general viscous isentropic compressible fluid occupying a spatial domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
with the mass density
\rho\geq 0
and the velocity
\textbf{u}=(u^{(1)},\cdots,u^{(N)})^{\top}

\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
is governed by the following isentropic compressible Navier-Stokes system (
CNS
):

(1.1)

\begin{cases}\rho_{t}+\dive(\rho\textbf{u})=0,\\[2.0pt]
(\rho\textbf{u})_{t}+\dive(\rho\textbf{u}\otimes\textbf{u})+\nabla P=\dive\mathbb{T}.\end{cases}

Here,
\textbf{y}=(y_{1},\cdots,y_{N})^{\top}\in\Omega
,
t\geq 0
are the space and time variables, respectively. For the polytropic gases, the constitutive relation is given by

(1.2)

P=A\rho^{\gamma},\quad A>0,\quad\gamma>1,

where
A
is an entropy constant and
\gamma
is the adiabatic exponent. The viscous stress tensor
\mathbb{T}
is given by

(1.3)

\mathbb{T}=2\mu(\rho)D(\textbf{u})+\lambda(\rho)\dive\textbf{u}\,\mathbb{I}_{N},

where
D(\textbf{u})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla\textbf{u}+(\nabla\textbf{u})^{\top}\right)
is the deformation tensor,
\mathbb{I}_{N}
is the
N\times N
identity matrix,

(1.4)

\mu(\rho)=a_{1}\rho^{\nu},\quad\lambda(\rho)=a_{2}\rho^{\nu},

for some constant
\nu\geq 0
,
\mu(\rho)
is the shear viscosity coefficient,
\lambda(\rho)+\frac{2}{N}\mu(\rho)
is the bulk viscosity coefficient,
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are both constants satisfying

(1.5)

a_{1}>0\quad\text{and}\quad 2a_{1}+Na_{2}\geq 0.

In the rarefied gas dynamics, the
CNS
can be derived from the Boltzmann equation through the Chapman-Enskog expansion, cf. Chapman-Cowling
[
9
]
and Li-Qin
[
40
]
. Under some proper physical assumptions, the viscosity coefficients
(\mu,\lambda)
and the heat conductivity coefficient
\kappa
are functions of the absolute temperature
\theta
. Actually,
for the cut-off inverse power force models (see
[
9
]
), if the intermolecular potential varies as
\ell^{-\varkappa}
,
where
\ell
is the intermolecular distance and
\varkappa
is a positive constant, then

(1.6)

\mu(\theta)=b_{1}\theta^{\frac{1}{2}+b},\quad\lambda(\theta)=b_{2}\theta^{\frac{1}{2}+b}\quad\text{and}\quad\kappa(\theta)=b_{3}\theta^{\frac{1}{2}+b}\quad\text{with}\quad b=\frac{2}{\varkappa}\in[0,\infty),

for some constants
(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})
.
In particular, for the ionized gas,

\varkappa=1
;
for Maxwellian molecules,

\varkappa=4
;
while for rigid elastic spherical molecules,

\varkappa=\infty
.
In Liu-Xin-Yang
[
44
]
, for isentropic and polytropic fluids, according to the laws of Boyle and Gay-Lussac:

P=\mathcal{R}\rho\theta=A\rho^{\gamma}\quad\text{for a constant }\mathcal{R}>0,

i.e.,
\theta=A\mathcal{R}^{-1}\rho^{\gamma-1}
, one finds that
(\mu,\lambda)
become functions of
\rho
taking the form
(\ref{fandan})
. Note that there exist other physical models satisfying the density-dependent viscosities assumption (
1.4
), such as the Korteweg system, the shallow water equations, the lake equations and the quantum Navier-Stokes system and so on (see
[
1
,
4
,
16
,
29
]
).

The current paper concerns the following vacuum free boundary problem (
VFBP
) for the one-dimensional (
1-D
) viscous Saint-Venant system for laminar shallow water,

(1.7)

\begin{cases}\rho_{t}+(\rho u)_{y}=0&\text{in}\ \ I(t),\\[2.0pt]
(\rho u)_{t}+(\rho u^{2}+\rho^{2})_{y}-(\rho u_{y})_{y}=0&\text{in}\ \ I(t),\\[2.0pt]
\rho>0&\text{in}\ \ I(t),\\[2.0pt]
\rho=0&\text{on}\ \ \Gamma(t),\\[2.0pt]
\mathcal{V}(\Gamma(t))=u&\text{on}\ \ \Gamma(t),\\[2.0pt]
(\rho,u)|_{t=0}=(\rho_{0},u_{0})&\text{on}\ \ I:=I(0)=(0,1),\end{cases}

where
\rho\geq 0
denotes the depth of the fluid,
u
the Eulerian velocity of the fluid, the open and bounded interval
I(t)\subset\mathbb{R}
the changing domain occupied by the fluid,
\Gamma(t):=\partial I(t)
the moving vacuum boundary,
y\in I(t)
the Eulerian spatial coordinate,
t\geq 0
the time coordinate, and
\mathcal{V}(\Gamma(t))
the velocity of
\Gamma(t)
, respectively. The viscous Saint-Venant system
\eqref{shallow}_{1}
-
\eqref{shallow}_{2}
in (
1.7
) can be derived rigorously from the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations with a free moving interface by Gerbeau-Perthame
[
16
]
, which corresponds to the degenerate
CNS
(
1.1
)-(
1.5
) with
N=\nu=A=1
,
a_{1}=1/2
,
a_{2}=0
and
\gamma=2
. Indeed, such models appear naturally and frequently in geophysical flows
[
1
,
4
]
. It is worth pointing out that the assumption that
(a_{1},A)=(1/2,1)
in
\eqref{shallow}_{1}
-
\eqref{shallow}_{2}
is just used to simplify the description in our analysis, and one can regard
(a_{1},A)
as any two positive constants.
\eqref{shallow}_{3}
states that there is no vacuum inside the fluid;
\eqref{shallow}_{4}
states that the depth vanishes along the moving vacuum boundary
\Gamma(t)
;
\eqref{shallow}_{5}
states that the vacuum boundary
\Gamma(t)
is moving with speed equal to the fluid velocity, and
\eqref{shallow}_{6}
provides the initial conditions for the depth, velocity, and domain.

The main goal here is to establish the local/global-in-time existence of the unique classical solution to problem (
1.7
) for general data with the initial depth profile such that

(1.8)

\rho_{0}^{\alpha}\in H^{3}(I)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathcal{C}_{1}d(y)\leq\rho_{0}^{\alpha}(y)\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}d(y)\ \ \text{for all }y\in\bar{I},

for some constants
\mathcal{C}_{1}>0
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}>0
and
0<\alpha\leq 1
, where
d(y):=\mathrm{dist}\,(y,\Gamma)
(
\Gamma:=\Gamma(0)
) is the distance function from
y\in\bar{I}
to
\Gamma
. It is interesting to note that the set of
\rho_{0}
defined by (
1.8
) contains two different kinds of initial profiles. Indeed, for
0<\alpha<1
, (
1.8
) implies that
\rho_{0}
satisfies the so-called BD entropy condition, i.e.,

(1.9)

\|(\sqrt{\rho_{0}})_{y}\|_{L^{2}(I)}<\infty,

which was initiated with a series of papers by Bresch-Desjardins
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
(started in 2003 with Lin
[
4
]
in the context of Navier-Stokes-Korteweg with a linear shear viscosity).
On the other hand, one denotes by

c=\sqrt{P^{\prime}(\rho)}

the speed of the sound,
c_{0}=c|_{t=0}
, and
n
the outward unit normal vector to the initial boundary. Then when
\alpha=1
,
\rho_{0}
satisfies the so-called physical vacuum boundary condition

(1.10)

-\infty<\frac{\partial c^{2}_{0}}{\partial\mathbf{n}}<0\quad\text{on}\ \Gamma,

which was first proposed by Liu
[
42
]
when he studied the self-similar solutions to compressible Euler with damping. This assumption means that
the pressure will induce a nontrivial finite acceleration in the outward normal direction on the moving boundary.
Furthermore, we can check that these two types of initial conditions on the depth shown in (
1.9
)-(
1.10
) are not compatible.

The study of the vacuum is crucial in the analysis of the dynamics of viscous compressible fluids (
[
5
,
18
,
28
,
41
,
50
,
59
]
). In fact, when
(\mu,\lambda,\kappa)
are all constants, some singular behaviors of solutions with vacuum to the Cauchy problem of
CNS
have already been observed. In particular, Hoff-Serre
[
25
]
shows that the weak solutions of the 1-D isentropic
CNS
need not depend continuously on their initial data when the initial density contains an interval of vacuum states; Li-Wang-Xin
[
34
]
proves the instantaneous blow up of
L^{2}
-norm of
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
solutions for
s>[\frac{N}{2}]+1
; and Xin-Zhu
[
61
]
and Duan-Xin-Zhu
[
14
]
prove that, for both the isentropic and non-isentropic flow, the classical solutions with vacuum of the three-dimensional (3-D)
CNS
cannot preserve the conservation of the momentum. These counterintuitive behaviors can be attributed to the unphysical assumption that
(\mu,\lambda,\kappa)
are all constants when one utilizes
CNS
to deal with the vacuum problems in fluids
[
44
]
, which indicates that the vacuum exerts a force on the fluid on the vacuum boundary. Thus, viscous compressible fluids near vacuum should be better modeled by the
CNS
with degenerate viscosities and heat conductivity, as was mentioned in (
1.4
)-(
1.6
).

However, for the isentropic
CNS
(
1.1
)-(
1.5
) with
\nu>0
, the momentum equations are degenerate both in the time evolution and spatial dissipation near the vacuum,

(1.11)

\displaystyle\underbrace{\rho(\textbf{u}_{t}+\textbf{u}\cdot\nabla\textbf{u})}_{\circledast}+\nabla P=\underbrace{\dive(\rho^{\nu}Q(\textbf{u}))}_{\Diamond},

where
\circledast
denotes the degenerate time evolution,
\Diamond
the degenerate dissipation, and
Q(\textbf{u})=2a_{1}D(\textbf{u})+a_{2}\text{div}\,\textbf{u}\,\mathbb{I}_{N}
.
Such a double degenerate structure in (
1.11
) may lead to singular behaviors of solutions compared with the uniformly parabolic systems, which makes it challenging to study the well-posedness of large solutions with vacuum. This degenerate system has attracted extensive attention recently, and
some important achievements both on weak and strong solutions with vacuum to its Cauchy problem have been obtained, cf.
[
6
,
21
,
37
,
57
,
38
,
61
,
60
,
8
,
19
,
39
]
.

It is worth pointing out that, for the important physical model, the shallow water equations that correspond to (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) with
\nu=1
and
\gamma=2
, the well-posedness theories of classical solutions in
[
8
,
38
]
allow vacuum only at far fields, and it is still unclear how to deal with the corresponding Cauchy problem with vacuum appearing in some open sets with nonzero measures. Actually, in the derivation of hydrodynamic equations from physical principles, the underlying assumption is that the fluid is non-dilute and can be described as a continuum, which means that one can not use hydrodynamic equations to study the time evolution of thermodynamical states in the vacuum region. Such considerations leads to studies on the vacuum problem for compressible fluids by the
VFBP
instead of the Cauchy problem, which arises in many important physical situations such as astrophysics, shallow water waves, etc., and has received much attention. For the
VFBP
of the isentropic compressible Euler equations ((
1.1
)-(
1.5
) with
a_{1}=a_{2}=0
), some significant progress on the well-posedness of smooth solutions satisfying (
1.10
) have been obtained. The local existence theory was developed by Coutand-Shkoller
[
12
,
13
]
and Jang-Masmoudi
[
26
,
27
]
,
and the unconditional uniqueness was proved by Luo-Xin-Zeng
[
45
]
. Recently, Jang-Hadžić
[
23
]
constructed global unique solutions when
\gamma\in(1,\frac{5}{3}]
, and the initial data lie sufficiently close to the expanding compactly supported affine motions constructed by Sideris
[
54
]
and they satisfy (
1.10
). We also refer readers to
[
11
,
20
,
47
,
43
,
51
,
56
]
and the references therein for some other related progress.

For the
VFBP
of the degenerate
CNS
, the key issue is whether the double degenerate structure shown in (
1.11
) can propagate the initial regularity of
u
, which is subtle and surprisingly different from the inviscid case. Actually, on the one hand, due to the appearance of the degenerate dissipation, the classical argument on the div-curl type estimates that is used in inviscid flows for establishing the normal estimates fails here. On the other hand, the viscosity degenerates at vacuum, which makes it difficult to adapt the standard regularity estimates theory of elliptic equations to the current case. Until now, only a few papers have addressed the well-posedness theory of strong or classical solutions to the
VFBP
of the degenerate isentropic
CNS
(
1.1
)-(
1.5
) and some related physical models. By taking the effect of gravity force into account, when the initial datum is a small perturbation of the steady solution, the global existence of the 1-D strong solution to (
1.10
) was proved by Ou-Zeng
[
52
]
. Later, under a suitable smallness assumption, Luo-Xin-Zeng
[
46
]
established the global existence of strong solutions to (
1.10
) of the 3-D spherically symmetric compressible Navier-Stokes-Poisson system with degenerate viscosities. Recently, assuming that
\rho_{0}\in H^{5}(I)
and
u_{0}
stays in a weighted
H^{6}(I)
space, Li-Wang-Xin
[
35
]
established the local well-posedness of classical solutions satisfying (
1.10
) to (
1.7
), and then extended this theory to the two-dimensional (2-D) shallow water equations in
[
36
]
under the assumption that
\rho_{0}\in H^{7}
and
u_{0}
stays in a weighted
H^{8}
space. Some other related progress can also be found in
[
17
,
22
,
58
,
62
]
and the references therein.

Despite these important progress on the
VFBP
for viscous compressible fluids, the global well-posedness of smooth solutions with large data is extremely difficult due to the degeneracies in the presence of the vacuum. Indeed, almost all the known results either on the local well-posedness with large data of classical solutions or global well-posedness for perturbed data of strong solutions to the
VFBP
of (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) (
[
35
,
36
,
46
,
52
]
) were obtained under the assumption of physical vacuum condition (
1.10
), which makes it possible to exclude the singularity formation near the vacuum boundary. Yet it seems difficult to generalize the techniques in
[
35
,
36
,
46
,
52
]
to the case in which (
1.10
) fails, or use them to establish global well-posedness of classical solutions even (
1.10
) is satisfied.
Due to the double degenerate structures in (
1.11
) in the presence of vacuum, it is challenging to establish global uniform estimates on high order derivatives in general unless some additional constraints, such as the BD entropy condition (
1.9
), are imposed. In fact, as far as we know, all the known theories for global well-posedness of strong or classical solutions to (
1.1
) with either general Cauchy data or initial boundary data on a fixed domain require that the initial density satisfies the BD entropy condition (
1.9
), see
[
8
,
10
,
24
,
49
]
. These seem to indicate that it is plausible to obtain the global well-posedness of classical solutions to the
VFBP
of (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) for general large data by exploiting the effects of both physical vacuum and BD entropy conditions. Unfortunately, such an approach fails to apply to our case since, as discussed earlier, for general initial density profiles satisfying (
1.8
), these two constraints are not compatible. Thus new ideas and techniques are needed to achieve the global well-posedness of classical solutions to the
VFBP
of (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) under either the physical vacuum condition or BD entropy condition alone (but not both). Fortunately, by exploiting the underlying intrinsic degenerate-singular structure of (
1.7
) and some elaborate analysis, we are able to identify a class of initial data defined in (
1.8
) for
\alpha\in\left(\frac{1}{3},1\right]
so that a global existence and uniqueness theory for classical solutions to the
VFBP
of (
1.1
)-(
1.5
) holds without restrictions on the size of the initial data.

For simplicity, in the rest of this paper, for any function space
X
appearing in this paper, unless otherwise specified,
X=X(I)
, and the following conventions are used:

\displaystyle H^{k}=W^{k,2},\quad H^{k}_{0}=W^{k,2}_{0},\quad H^{-k}=(H^{k}_{0})^{*},\quad\int f\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}f\,\mathrm{d}x,

\displaystyle\left|f\right|_{p}=\left\|f\right\|_{L^{p}},\quad\left\|f\right\|_{m,p}=\left\|f\right\|_{W^{m,p}},\quad\left\|f\right\|_{s}=\left\|f\right\|_{H^{s}},

\displaystyle H^{k}_{\omega^{p}}=\big\{f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}:\omega^{\frac{p}{2}}\partial_{x}^{j}f\in L^{2},\,\,0\leq j\leq k\big\},\quad L^{2}_{\omega^{p}}=H^{0}_{\omega^{p}},\quad H^{-k}_{\omega^{p}}:=(H^{k}_{\omega^{p}})^{*},

\displaystyle\left|f\right|_{2,\omega^{p}}=\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\omega^{p}}}=\big|\omega^{\frac{p}{2}}f\big|_{2},\ \ \left\|f\right\|_{k,\omega^{p}}=\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\omega^{p}}}=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\big|\omega^{\frac{p}{2}}\partial_{x}^{j}f\big|_{2},

\displaystyle d(x)\!:=\mathrm{dist}(x,\Gamma),\quad X([0,T];Y)=X([0,T];Y(I)),

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{X_{t}(Y)}=\left\|f\right\|_{X([0,T];Y)},\ \ \|\varphi(f,g)\|_{X}=\|\varphi f\|_{X}+\|\varphi g\|_{X},

where
\omega\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}
stands for a generic weight function. Moreover, we denote by
X^{*}
the dual space of
X
. More details on weighted Sobolev spaces can be found in Kufner
[
32
]
.

1.1.
Main results in Lagrangian coordinates

Denote by
\eta(t,x)
the position of the fluid particle
x\in I
at time
t
so that

(1.12)

\eta_{t}(t,x)=u(t,\eta(t,x))\ \ \text{for}\ t>0\ \ \text{and}\ \ \eta(0,x)=x,

and
(t,x)
are the Lagrangian coordinates. Set

(1.13)

H(t,x):=\rho(t,\eta(t,x)),\quad U(t,x):=u(t,\eta(t,x)).

Then the
VFBP
(
1.7
) can be rewritten into the following initial-boundary value problem in the fixed domain
I
in Lagrangian coordinates
(t,x)
:

(1.14)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle H_{t}+H\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}}=0&\text{in}\ \ (0,T]\times I,\\[2.0pt]
\displaystyle\eta_{x}HU_{t}+(H^{2})_{x}-\left(H\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}}\right)_{x}=0&\text{in}\ \ (0,T]\times I,\\[2.0pt]
\displaystyle\eta_{t}=U&\text{in}\ \ (0,T]\times I,\\[2.0pt]
H>0&\text{in}\ \ (0,T]\times I,\\[2.0pt]
H=0&\text{on}\ \ (0,T]\times\Gamma,\\[2.0pt]
(H,U,\eta)=(\rho_{0},u_{0},\mathrm{id})&\text{on}\ \ \{t=0\}\times I.\end{cases}

\eqref{lagrange}_{1}
and
\eqref{lagrange}_{3}
imply that

(1.15)

H(t,x)=\frac{\rho_{0}(x)}{\eta_{x}(t,x)}.

Thus (
1.14
) becomes the following initial boundary value problem for
(U,\eta)
,

(1.16)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\rho_{0}U_{t}+\left(\frac{\rho_{0}^{2}}{\eta_{x}^{2}}\right)_{x}-\left(\frac{\rho_{0}U_{x}}{\eta_{x}^{2}}\right)_{x}=0&\text{in}\ \ (0,T]\times I,\\[2.0pt]
\displaystyle\eta_{t}=U&\text{in}\ \ (0,T]\times I,\\[2.0pt]
(U,\eta)=(u_{0},\mathrm{id})&\text{on}\ \ \{t=0\}\times I.\end{cases}

The classical solutions to (
1.16
) can be defined as follows.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
T
be any positive number.
(U(t,x),\eta(t,x))
is called a classical solution in
[0,T]\times\bar{I}
to problem (
1.16
), if

\displaystyle U\in C([0,T];C^{2}(\bar{I}))\cap C^{1}([0,T];C(\bar{I})),\ \ \eta\in C^{1}([0,T];C^{2}(\bar{I}))\cap C^{2}([0,T];C(\bar{I})),

satisfy the equations
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{1}
-
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{2}
pointwise in
(0,T]\times I
, and take the initial data
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{3}
continuously.

In order to construct classical solutions to (
1.16
), we consider the following two types of high-order energy functions:

(1.17)

\displaystyle E(t,U)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=0}^{2}\big|\rho_{0}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{k}U(t)\big|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{1}\big|\rho_{0}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{k}U_{x}(t)\big|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left|\rho_{0}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}U_{xx}(t)\right|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{k=2}^{4}\big|\rho_{0}^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{k}U(t)\big|_{2}^{2},

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}(t,U)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=0}^{2}\big|\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{t}^{k}U(t)\big|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{1}\big|\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{t}^{k}U_{x}(t)\big|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\big|\rho_{0}^{\left(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}\right)\alpha}\partial_{t}U_{xx}(t)\big|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{k=2}^{4}\big|\rho_{0}^{\left(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}\right)\alpha}\partial_{x}^{k}U(t)\big|_{2}^{2},

where

(1.18)

\begin{cases}0<\varepsilon_{0}\leq\frac{3\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\text{ and }0<\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{1}{\alpha}-1&\text{for }\frac{1}{3}<\alpha<1,\\
0<\varepsilon_{0}<1&\text{for }\alpha=1.\end{cases}

In addition, we define the following spaces: for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\begin{split}\mathscr{C}^{\ell}([0,T];E):=&\left\{F\left|\begin{array}[]{cc}\rho_{0}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{j}F,\,\,\rho_{0}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{k}F_{x}\in C^{\ell}([0,T];L^{2}),\quad j=0,1,2,\,\,k=0,1.\\[4.0pt]
\rho_{0}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}F_{xx},\,\,\rho_{0}^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{j}F\in C^{\ell}([0,T];L^{2}),\quad j=2,3,4.\end{array}\right.\right\},\\
\mathscr{C}^{\ell}([0,T];\widetilde{E}):=&\Bigg\{F\Bigg|\begin{array}[]{cc}\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{t}^{j}F,\,\,\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{t}^{k}F_{x}\in C^{\ell}([0,T];L^{2}),\quad j=0,1,2,\,\,k=0,1.\\[4.0pt]
\rho_{0}^{\left(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}\right)\alpha}\partial_{t}F_{xx},\,\,\rho_{0}^{\left(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}\right)\alpha}\partial_{x}^{j}F\in C^{\ell}([0,T];L^{2}),\quad j=2,3,4.\end{array}\Bigg.\Bigg\}.\end{split}

Now, we are ready to state the main results in Lagrangian coordinates. The first one is the local-in-time existence of the unique classical solution to (
1.16
).

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume that (
1.8
) holds for
0<\alpha\leq 1
.

\mathrm{i)}

If
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{3}
and
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfies

(1.19)

E(0,U)<\infty,

then there exist a time
T_{*}>0
and a unique classical solution
(U,\eta)
in
[0,T_{*}]\times\bar{I}
to (
1.16
) such that

(1.20)

\begin{gathered}U\in\mathscr{C}([0,T_{*}];E),\quad\eta\in\mathscr{C}^{1}([0,T_{*}];E);\\
\frac{1}{2}\leq\eta_{x}(t,x)\leq\frac{3}{2}\ \ \text{for all }(t,x)\in[0,T_{*}]\times\bar{I}.\end{gathered}

In particular,

(1.21)

\begin{gathered}\ \quad\quad\quad U\in C([0,T_{*}];H^{3})\cap C^{1}([0,T_{*}];H^{1}),\ \eta\in C^{1}([0,T_{*}];H^{3})\cap C^{2}([0,T_{*}];H^{1}).\end{gathered}

Moreover, such a classical solution admits the following Neumann boundary condition,

(1.22)

U_{x}(t,x)=0\ \ \text{on}\ (0,T_{*}]\times\Gamma,

and the asymptotic behavior,

(1.23)

\left|U_{x}(t,x)\right|\leq Cd(x)\ \ \text{in}\ (0,T_{*}]\times\bar{I}.

\mathrm{ii)}

If
\frac{1}{3}<\alpha\leq 1
and
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfies

(1.24)

\widetilde{E}(0,U)<\infty,

then there exist a time
T_{*}>0
and a unique classical solution
(U,\eta)
in
[0,T_{*}]\times\bar{I}
to (
1.16
) such that

(1.25)

\begin{gathered}U\in\mathscr{C}([0,T_{*}];\widetilde{E}),\quad\eta\in\mathscr{C}^{1}([0,T_{*}];\widetilde{E});\\
\frac{1}{2}\leq\eta_{x}(t,x)\leq\frac{3}{2}\ \ \text{for all }(t,x)\in[0,T_{*}]\times\bar{I}.\end{gathered}

Moreover, (
1.22
)-(
1.23
) hold, and

(1.26)

\begin{gathered}U\in C([0,T_{*}];W^{3,1})\cap C^{1}([0,T_{*}];W^{1,1}),\\
\eta\in C^{1}([0,T_{*}];W^{3,1})\cap C^{2}([0,T_{*}];W^{1,1}).\end{gathered}

The second one is the global-in-time existence of the unique classical solution with large data to (
1.16
).

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume that
\frac{1}{3}<\alpha\leq 1
and
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfies (
1.8
) and (
1.24
).
Then for arbitrarily large time
T>0
, there exists a unique classical solution
(U,\eta)
in
[0,T]\times\bar{I}
to (
1.16
), satisfying

(1.27)

\begin{gathered}U\in\mathscr{C}([0,T];\widetilde{E}),\quad\eta\in\mathscr{C}^{1}([0,T];\widetilde{E});\\
C^{-1}(T)\leq\eta_{x}(t,x)\leq C(T)\ \ \text{for all }(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\bar{I},\end{gathered}

where
C(T)
is a positive constant depending only on
\alpha
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
|I|
,
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
and
T
.
Moreover, (
1.22
)-(
1.23
) and (
1.26
) hold with
T_{*}
replaced by
T
.

1.2.
Main results in Eulerian coordinates

Denote
\mathbb{I}(T)=\{(t,y)|t\in(0,T],\ y\in I(t)\}
. The classical solutions to the
VFBP
(
1.7
) in
\overline{\mathbb{I}(T)}
can be defined as follows.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
T
be any positive number. A triple
(\rho(t,y),u(t,y),\Gamma(t))
is said to be a classical solution to the
VFBP
(
1.7
) in
\overline{\mathbb{I}(T)}
, if

\begin{split}\rho,\ \rho_{t},\ \rho_{y},\ u,\ u_{y},\ u_{yy},\ u_{t}\in C(\overline{\mathbb{I}(T)}),\ \Gamma(t)\in C^{2}([0,T]),\end{split}

(\rho,u,\Gamma)
satisfies the equations
\eqref{shallow}_{1}
-
\eqref{shallow}_{3}
pointwise in
\mathbb{I}(T)
, takes the initial data
\eqref{shallow}_{6}
, and satisfies the boundary conditions
\eqref{shallow}_{4}
-
\eqref{shallow}_{5}
continuously.

Now, the main results shown in the previous subsection can be given in Eulerian coordinates as follows.

Theorem 1.3
.

Assume that (
1.8
) and (
1.19
) hold for
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{3}
; while (
1.8
) and (
1.24
) hold for
\frac{1}{3}<\alpha\leq 1
. Then there exist a time
T_{*}>0
and a unique classical solution
(\rho(t,y),u(t,y),\Gamma(t))
in
\overline{\mathbb{I}(T_{*})}
to the
VFBP
(
1.7
) such that for
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{3}
,

(1.28)

\begin{split}\sup_{t\in[0,T_{*}]}\big(\|\rho^{\alpha}\|_{H^{3}(I(t))}+\|(\rho^{\alpha})_{t}\|_{H^{2}(I(t))}+\|u\|_{H^{3}(I(t))}+\|u_{t}\|_{H^{1}(I(t))}\big)<\infty,\end{split}

while for

\frac{1}{3}<\alpha\leq 1
,

(1.29)

\begin{split}\sup_{t\in[0,T_{*}]}\big(\|\rho^{\alpha}\|_{W^{3,1}(I(t))}+\|(\rho^{\alpha})_{t}\|_{W^{2,1}(I(t))}+\|u\|_{W^{3,1}(I(t))}+\|u_{t}\|_{W^{1,1}(I(t))}\big)<\infty.\end{split}

Moreover, the velocity satisfies

(1.30)

u_{y}(t,y)=0\ \ \text{for all }t\in[0,T_{*}]\text{ and }y\in\Gamma(t).

Theorem 1.4
.

Assume that
\frac{1}{3}<\alpha\leq 1
, (
1.8
) and (
1.24
) hold. Then for arbitrarily large time
T>0
, there exists a unique classical solution
(\rho(t,y),u(t,y),\Gamma(t))
in
\overline{\mathbb{I}(T)}
to the
VFBP
(
1.7
). Moreover, (
1.29
)-(
1.30
) hold with
T_{*}
replaced by
T
.

Furthermore, in contrast to the classical theory
[
31
,
48
]
, if
|\int_{I}\rho_{0}u_{0}\mathrm{d}y|>0
, the velocity of solutions
obtained in Theorem
1.4
does not decay to zero in the
L^{\infty}
norm as
t\rightarrow\infty
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Assume that
|\int_{I}\rho_{0}u_{0}\mathrm{d}y|>0
.
Then the global classical solution
(\rho,u)
to (
1.7
) obtained in Theorem
1.4
does not
satisfy

(1.31)

\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{y\in I(t)}|u(t,y)|=0.

We make some comments on the results of this paper.

Remark 1.1
.

It should be noted that (
1.22
) in the case
\alpha=1
has been observed in
[
35
,
36
]
. Now we show how to derive (
1.22
) in the general case. Take the case
\frac{1}{3}<\alpha\leq 1
for example. First, it follows from (
1.8
), (
1.12
), (
1.26
) and Lemma
A.4
that

(1.32)

\begin{gathered}\rho_{0}^{\alpha}\in C^{2}(\bar{I}),\ U_{x},\ U_{xx},\ U_{t},\ \eta_{x},\ \eta_{xx}\in C([0,T_{*}]\times\bar{I}).\end{gathered}

Next, multiplying both sides of
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{1}
by
\eta_{x}^{2}\rho_{0}^{\alpha-1}
gives

(1.33)

\rho_{0}^{\alpha}U_{xx}=\frac{2}{\alpha}\rho_{0}(\rho_{0}^{\alpha})_{x}+\rho_{0}^{\alpha}\eta_{x}^{2}U_{t}-\frac{2\rho_{0}^{\alpha+1}\eta_{xx}}{\eta_{x}}-\frac{1}{\alpha}(\rho_{0}^{\alpha})_{x}U_{x}+\frac{2\rho_{0}^{\alpha}\eta_{xx}U_{x}}{\eta_{x}}.

Then letting
x
approach the boundary
\Gamma
in (
1.33
), one obtains from (
1.25
) and (
1.32
) that

(1.34)

(\rho_{0}^{\alpha})_{x}U_{x}=0\ \ \text{for }(t,x)\in(0,T_{*}]\times\Gamma,

which, along with

(\rho_{0}^{\alpha})_{x}|_{x\in\Gamma}\neq 0
,
yields that
U_{x}=0
for
(t,x)\in(0,T_{*}]\times\Gamma
. It is worth noting that (
1.22
) can be thought of as inheriting from the homogeneous Neumann boundary condition of
u_{0}
which is shown in Lemma
B.1
, and (
1.22
) will play an important role in establishing the uniform lower and upper bounds of
\eta_{x}
in §
6
.

Remark 1.2
.

For the
VFBP
(
1.7
), it follows from (
1.8
), (
1.22
) and (
1.26
) that the usual
stress free boundary condition holds automatically, i.e.,

\mathbb{S}=\rho^{2}-\rho u_{y}=0\ \ \text{for }t\in(0,T]\text{ and }y\in\Gamma(t).

Remark 1.3
.

The initial assumptions (
1.8
) and (
1.19
) or (
1.24
) in Theorem
1.1
identify a class of admissible initial data that ensure unique solvability of (
1.16
). In Appendix
B
, we give an equivalent form of (
1.19
) or (
1.24
) in terms of
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
themselves and their spatial derivatives in Lemma
B.1
. Indeed, it follows from Lemma
B.1
that the assumptions (
1.8
) and (
1.19
) or (
1.24
) in Theorem
1.1
can be fulfilled if
\rho^{\alpha}_{0}\in H^{3}
and
u_{0}
stays in a weighted
H^{4}
space and a special compatibility condition is satisfied. In particular, Lemma
B.1
implies the following facts.

First, for the case
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{3}
, (
1.8
) and (
1.19
) can be satisfied by:

(1.35)

\rho_{0}(x):=C\left(x(1-x)\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}},\quad u_{0}(x)\in C^{\infty}_{c},

where
C>0
denotes a generic constant. The details can be found in Remark
B.1
.

Second, for
\frac{1}{3}<\alpha\leq 1
, (
1.8
) and (
1.24
) are fulfilled by the set of initial data given by

(1.36)

\rho_{0}(x):=C\left(x(1-x)\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}},\quad u_{0}(x):=\int_{0}^{x}\rho_{0}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z+f_{0}(x),

for arbitrary
f_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(I)
. The details can be found in Remark
B.1
.

We make some comments on the methodology of this paper.

Remark 1.4
.

We give some comments on the forms of energy functions
(E(t,U),\widetilde{E}(t,U))
in (
1.17
). In order to get the solution which is classical uniformly up to the moving boundary, inspired by the Sobolev embedding theorem and the Hardy inequality, we should establish some weighted
H^{4}
estimates on
U
. So a natural energy function takes the form

(1.37)

E^{*}(t,U)=\sum_{k=0}^{2}\big|\rho_{0}^{p}\partial_{t}^{k}U(t)\big|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{1}\big|\rho_{0}^{p}\partial_{t}^{k}U_{x}(t)\big|_{2}^{2}+\left|\rho_{0}^{q}\partial_{t}U_{xx}(t)\right|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{k=2}^{4}\big|\rho_{0}^{q}\partial_{x}^{k}U(t)\big|_{2}^{2}.

To derive the highest order elliptic estimates, by formally applying
\rho_{0}^{q-\alpha}\partial_{t}
and
\rho_{0}^{q-\alpha}\partial_{x}^{2}
, respectively, to both sides of (
1.33
), one has

(1.38)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}^{q}\partial_{t}U_{xx}

\displaystyle=\rho_{0}^{q}\eta_{x}^{2}U_{tt}+(R_{q}^{1}),

\displaystyle\rho_{0}^{q}\partial_{x}^{4}U

\displaystyle=\frac{2(1-\alpha)}{\alpha^{3}}\rho_{0}^{1-3\alpha+q}(\rho_{0}^{\alpha})_{x}^{3}+\rho_{0}^{q}\eta_{x}^{2}\partial_{t}U_{xx}+(R_{q}^{2}),

which, by substituting
\eqref{Utxx-Uxxxx}_{1}
into
\eqref{Utxx-Uxxxx}_{2}
, leads to

(1.39)

\rho_{0}^{q}\partial_{x}^{4}U=\underline{\frac{2(1-\alpha)}{\alpha^{3}}\rho_{0}^{1-3\alpha+q}(\rho_{0}^{\alpha})_{x}^{3}}_{(\star_{1})}+\underline{\rho_{0}^{q}\eta_{x}^{4}U_{tt}}_{(\star_{2})}+(R_{q}^{3}),

where
((R_{q}^{1}),(R_{q}^{2}),(R_{q}^{3}))
denote the remaining terms. As can be checked,
(\star_{1})
is the most singular part of the derivatives of the pressure,
(\star_{2})
is the highest-order tangential derivatives, and
(R_{q}^{3})
, compared with
(\star_{1})
-
(\star_{2})
, possesses either higher-order weights or lower-order derivatives of
U
. It turns out that, to control
|\rho_{0}^{q}\partial_{x}^{4}U|_{2}
by (
1.39
), the main obstacles are
(\star_{1})
-
(\star_{2})
.
Hence it follows from (
1.8
) that
(\star_{1})
-
(\star_{2})
belong to
L^{2}
whenever

(1.40)

q>\frac{5\alpha}{2}-1\ \text{ or }\ \alpha=1,\ \ \text{ and }\ q\geq p.

On the other hand, the embedding relation
H^{4}_{\rho_{0}^{2\alpha}}\hookrightarrow H^{3}\hookrightarrow C^{2}(\bar{I})
(see Lemmas
A.4
-
A.5
in Appendix
A
) implies that
q\leq\alpha
in (
1.39
). Therefore, in order to get the maximum range of
\alpha
, we determine the energy function
E(t,U)
in (
1.17
) by setting
p=q=\alpha
in (
1.37
), and finally obtain from (
1.40
) that
\alpha\in\left(0,\frac{2}{3}\right)
or
\alpha=1
. Fortunately, it follows from direct calculations that
|(R_{q}^{3})|_{2}
can be controlled by
E(t,U)
with the above well-chosen weights.

When
\alpha\in\left[\frac{2}{3},1\right)
, based on Lemmas
A.4
-
A.5
, we consider the embedding relation
H^{4}_{\rho_{0}^{2q}}\hookrightarrow W^{3,1}\hookrightarrow C^{2}(\bar{I})
for
q<\frac{3\alpha}{2}
. On the other hand, it seems that one can determine
p=\frac{1}{2}
in (
1.37
) from establishing the tangential estimates via
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{1}
, and hence it follows from (
1.40
) and
q<\frac{3\alpha}{2}
that the maximum range of
\alpha
is
\alpha\in\left(\frac{1}{3},1\right]
. As can be checked,
|(R_{q}^{3})|_{2}
is controlled by
\widetilde{E}(t,U)
with such a weight. Actually, only from the perspective of the local existence, one can still obtain the tangential estimates with
p\neq\frac{1}{2}
via a simple reformulation on
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{1}
by multiplying both of its sides by
\rho_{0}^{2p-1}
, while such a reformulation will break some intrinsic structure of
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{1}
and make it hard to establish the global-in-time energy estimates. Therefore, we determine the energy function
\widetilde{E}(t,U)
in (
1.17
) by setting
p=\frac{1}{2}
and
q<\frac{3\alpha}{2}
in (
1.37
), then deduce from (
1.40
) that
\frac{1}{3}<\alpha\leq 1
.

Based on the above considerations, we will establish the desired solutions in two different energy functions:
E(t,U)
when
\alpha\in\left(0,\frac{1}{3}\right]
, and
\widetilde{E}(t,U)
when
\alpha\in\left(\frac{1}{3},1\right]
.

Remark 1.5
.

For proving the local existence stated in Theorem
1.1
, a key step is to establish the existence and uniqueness of the corresponding linearized problems (
3.1
)-(
3.2
) in §
3
via the Galerkin method. However, the standard Galerkin method (see
[
15
]
) is not applicable here, since there will be some issue that arises in the approximation of initial data
U_{tt}(0,x)
, which only belongs to
L^{2}_{\rho_{0}}
, via the standard Hilbert basis
\{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
which is orthonormal in
L^{2}
and orthogonal in
H^{1}
generated by the Laplace operator. To solve this problem, we apply a modified Galerkin method by using a sequence of
\{\ddot{U}^{\delta}_{0}\}_{\delta>0}\subset C^{\infty}(\bar{I})
to approximate
U_{tt}(0,x)
, such that each
\ddot{U}^{\delta}_{0}
can be expanded by
\{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
, then considering the linearized problem with the initial data
\ddot{U}^{\delta}_{0}
, and finally recovering the original linearized problems (
3.1
)-(
3.2
) by the standard density arguments. Note that this issue has been initially noticed by Li-Wang-Xin
[
35
,
36
]
when
\alpha=1
, where they developed a different approach to overcome the difficulties by constructing a new Galerkin basis
\{w_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
which is orthonormal in
L^{2}_{\rho_{0}}
and orthogonal in
H^{1}_{\rho_{0}}
. More details can be found in §
2.2
.

Remark 1.6
.

As mentioned before, the BD entropy condition (
1.9
) and physical vacuum condition (
1.10
) are not compatible in (
1.8
). Then for the case
\alpha=1
, since (
1.9
) fails, the classical global-in-time estimates on the first order derivatives of the depth obtained in
[
1
,
3
,
2
,
4
,
30
]
are not available here.
Yet we can still establish the global existence and uniqueness of classical solutions to the
VFBP
(
1.7
) with large data for
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{3},1]
. The key tool used here is the so-called effective velocity
V=U+\frac{H_{x}}{\rho_{0}}
. By taking full advantage of the transport mechanism of the evolution equation (
6.6
) of
V
, we exploit some new global weighted
L^{p}
(
2\leq p\leq\infty
) estimates for
V
(see Lemmas
6.5
and
6.6
),
which do not require the initial BD entropy condition or the physical vacuum boundary condition.
Such new estimates enable one to deal effectively with the terms related with
H_{x}
and
\eta_{xx}
that appear in the lower order estimates of
U
, and then establish the global-in-time upper bound of
\eta_{x}
and the weighted energy estimates of
U
. More details can be found in §
2.2
.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In §
2
, we first introduce a new reformulation of (
1.16
) in §
2.1
, and then outline the main strategy to establish the local/global existence and uniqueness. §
3
-§
5
are devoted to proving the local existence and uniqueness of classical solutions to problem (
1.16
) and hence the
VFBP
(
1.7
) as follows:

(1)

construct global smooth approximate solutions for the corresponding degenerate linearized problems via the Galerkin method (§
3
);

(2)

establish the uniform weighted estimates for the linearized problems (§
4
);

(3)

establish the local existence and uniqueness of classical solutions to the nonlinear problems (
1.16
) and (
1.7
), respectively, through the classical Picard iteration (§
5
).

In §
6
-§
8
, we show the global existence of classical solutions to the problem (
1.16
) and the
VFBP
(
1.7
) in the following three steps:

(1)

derive the global-in-time a priori lower and upper bounds for
\eta_{x}
, and the weighted boundedness of the effective velocity (§
6
);

(2)

establish the global-in-time a priori weighted estimates for the velocity (§
7
);

(3)

obtain the global existence and uniqueness of classical solutions to the nonlinear problems (
1.16
) and (
1.7
), respectively, by the standard continuity method (§
8
).

The non-decay result stated in Theorem
1.5
is proved in §
9
.
Finally, for the convenience of readers, we list some basic facts and auxiliary lemmas which have been used frequently in this paper in Appendixes A-E.

2.
Reformulations and the main strategy

In this section, we first reformulate
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{1}
according to the value of
\alpha
, and then sketch the main strategy of the analysis. Throughout the rest of this paper,
C(\geq 1)
will denote
a generic constant which depends only on fixed constants
\alpha
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
|I|
and
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
, which may be different from line to line; we will also use
C(\nu_{1},\cdots,\nu_{k})
to emphasize the dependency of
C
on the additional parameters
\nu_{1},\cdots,\nu_{k}
;
A\sim B
means
C^{-1}A\leq B\leq CA
; and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the inner product in
L^{2}
.

2.1.
Reformulations

Set
\phi_{0}:=\rho_{0}^{\alpha}
, and rewrite (
1.16
) into the following two forms:

•

for
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{3}
,

(2.1)

\begin{cases}\quad\displaystyle\phi_{0}^{2}U_{t}-\left(\frac{\phi_{0}^{2}U_{x}}{\eta_{x}^{2}}\right)_{x}\\
\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha}-2\Big)\frac{\phi_{0}(\phi_{0})_{x}U_{x}}{\eta_{x}^{2}}-\bigg(\frac{\phi_{0}^{2+\frac{1}{\alpha}}}{\eta_{x}^{2}}\bigg)_{x}+\Big(2-\frac{1}{\alpha}\Big)\frac{\phi_{0}^{1+\frac{1}{\alpha}}(\phi_{0})_{x}}{\eta_{x}^{2}}&\text{in }(0,T]\times I,\\
\quad\displaystyle\eta_{t}=U&\text{in}\ \ (0,T]\times I,\\
\quad(U,\eta)=(u_{0},\mathrm{id})&\text{on }\{t=0\}\times I;\end{cases}

•

for
\frac{1}{3}<\alpha\leq 1
,

(2.2)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\phi_{0}^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}U_{t}-\bigg(\frac{\phi_{0}^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}U_{x}}{\eta_{x}^{2}}\bigg)_{x}+\bigg(\frac{\phi_{0}^{\frac{2}{\alpha}}}{\eta_{x}^{2}}\bigg)_{x}=0&\text{in }(0,T]\times I,\\
\displaystyle\displaystyle\eta_{t}=U&\text{in}\ \ (0,T]\times I,\\
(U,\eta)=(u_{0},\mathrm{id})&\text{on }\{t=0\}\times I.\end{cases}

Under the above reformulations, the condition (
1.8
) is equivalent to

(2.3)

\phi_{0}\in H^{3}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathcal{C}_{1}d(x)\leq\phi_{0}(x)\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}d(x)\quad\text{for all }x\in I,

and the energy functionals (
1.17
) can be rewritten as

(2.4)

\displaystyle E(t,U)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{2}|\phi_{0}\partial_{t}^{k}U(t)|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{1}|\phi_{0}\partial_{t}^{k}U_{x}(t)|_{2}^{2}+\left|\phi_{0}\partial_{t}U_{xx}(t)\right|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{k=2}^{4}|\phi_{0}\partial_{x}^{k}U(t)|_{2}^{2},

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}(t,U)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{2}\big|\phi_{0}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\partial_{t}^{k}U(t)\big|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{1}\big|\phi_{0}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\partial_{t}^{k}U_{x}(t)\big|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\big|\phi_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\partial_{t}U_{xx}(t)\big|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{k=2}^{4}\big|\phi_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}\partial_{x}^{k}U(t)\big|_{2}^{2}.

2.2.
Main strategy

Our main strategy and new ideas will be given in this section. To overcome the difficulty caused by the degeneracy and singular behavior near the vacuum free boundary for large data problems, the main ingredients of the analysis consist of

•

some new weighted energy functionals;

•

a modified Galerkin scheme for local existence;

•

a new cross-derivatives embedding theorem for the elliptic estimates;

•

some new global weighted
L^{p}
(
2\leq p\leq\infty
) estimates for the effective velocity (that are different from the classical ones in Bresch-Desjardins
[
1
]

and Kanel
[
30
]
), which are crucial for the case
\alpha=1
in (
1.8
) since the initial BD entropy condition fails;

•

the global-in-time lower and upper bounds of
\eta_{x}
for large data problems that are given by some novel
(\rho_{0},\eta_{x})
-weighted estimates for
U_{x}
.

2.2.1.
Some new weighted energy functionals

The strong degeneracy of the momentum equation
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{1}

makes it intricate
to provide an effective propagation mechanism for the regularity of
U
near the vacuum in general Sobolev spaces. The first key step in constructing classical solutions is to introduce suitable weighted energy
functionals.
By considering the balance of the pressure and the spatial dissipation near the vacuum, we give
E(t,U)
for
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{3}]
and
\widetilde{E}(t,U)
for
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{3},1]
by (
1.17
), which turn out to be reasonable later. The details on how to construct these energy functionals can be found in Remark
1.4
.

2.2.2.
A modified Galerkin scheme

Starting from
(E(t,U),\widetilde{E}(t,U))
, for the local existence, we introduce
a modified Galerkin scheme to overcome the degeneracy of the time evolution. Actually, the classical Galerkin scheme does not work here. We only take the case
\alpha=1
for example to show this issue, and the other cases can be dealt with similarly.

Usually, one may choose a smooth and orthogonal basis
\{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
of
H^{1}
with
(e_{j})_{x}|_{x\in\Gamma}=0
, which is orthonormal in
L^{2}
, and the Galerkin approximate solutions have the form
X^{n}(t,x)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{j}^{n}(t)e_{j}(x)
with
\mu^{n}_{j}(0)=\langle u_{0},e_{j}\rangle
(see
[
15
]
). For obtaining the local existence in
\widetilde{E}(t,U)
to (
3.2
), the classical Galerkin method will first establish the uniform energy estimates for
X^{n}
, then obtain the unique weak solution
U
of (
3.2
), and finally repeat this process sequentially for
(X^{n}_{x},X^{n}_{t},X^{n}_{tx},X^{n}_{tt})
to improve the tangential regularities for
U
. However,
a question naturally arises when establishing such kind of energy estimate:
does there exist a constant
C>0
, independent of
n
, such that

(2.5)

\sum_{k=0}^{2}\big|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\partial_{t}^{k}X^{n}(0)\big|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{1}\big|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\partial_{t}^{k}X^{n}_{x}(0)\big|_{2}^{2}\leq C\widetilde{E}(0,U)\,?

For example,
X^{n}_{tt}(0)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\mu_{j}^{n})^{\prime\prime}(0)e_{j}
is defined by the equation of
\mu_{j}^{n}
at
t=0
, while the form of
(\mu_{j}^{n})^{\prime\prime}(0)
is too complicated to obtain (
2.5
). In fact, a similar issue has been noticed in
[
35
,
36
]
, where they constructed a Hilbert basis that is orthogonal in a suitable weighted Sobolev space, so that (
2.5
) can be derived directly via Bessel’s inequality.

In the current paper, we developed a new way to establish the regularities of
(U,U_{t},U_{tt})
by a modified Galerkin scheme. Specifically, we consider three
linear problems: the linear problem (
3.2
) for
U=w^{(0)}
itself, the linear problem for
U_{t}=w^{(1)}
:

(2.6)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\rho_{0}w^{(1)}_{t}-\bigg(\frac{\rho_{0}w^{(1)}_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}\bigg)_{x}=\bigg(\frac{2\rho_{0}^{2}\bar{U}_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{3}}-\frac{2\rho_{0}\bar{U}_{x}U_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{3}}\bigg)_{x}&\text{in }(0,T]\times I,\\
w^{(1)}=U_{t}(0,x)&\text{on }\{t=0\}\times I,\end{cases}

and the linear problem for
U_{tt}=w^{(2)}
:

(2.7)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\quad\rho_{0}w^{(2)}_{t}-\bigg(\frac{\rho_{0}w^{(2)}_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}\bigg)_{x}\\[8.0pt]
\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2\rho_{0}^{2}\bar{U}_{tx}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{3}}-\frac{6\rho_{0}^{2}\bar{U}_{x}^{2}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{4}}-\frac{2\rho_{0}\bar{U}_{tx}U_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{3}}-\frac{4\rho_{0}\bar{U}_{x}U_{tx}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{3}}+\frac{6\rho_{0}\bar{U}_{x}^{2}U_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{4}}\right)_{x}&\text{in }(0,T]\times I,\\
\quad w^{(2)}=U_{tt}(0,x)&\text{on }\{t=0\}\times I,\end{cases}

where
(\bar{\eta},\bar{U})
are some known functions.

\mathrm{i)}

first, we prove the global existence of weak solutions to (
3.2
), (
2.6
) and (
2.7
) in
L^{2}_{\rho_{0}}
space by the Galerkin method, respectively;

\mathrm{ii)}

second, since
(w^{(1)},w^{(2)})
have the same regularity as
w^{(0)}
as a weak solution in
L^{2}_{\rho_{0}}
space, the issue for deriving the regularities of
(U_{t},U_{tt})
is naturally converted into proving
(w^{(1)},w^{(2)})=(U_{t},U_{tt})
.

On the one hand, for the theory in
{\rm i)}
, we just take (
2.7
) for example, and (
3.2
), (
2.6
) can be solved similarly.
We first choose a sequence of smooth functions
\{\ddot{U}_{0}^{\delta}\}_{\delta>0}
that converges to
U_{tt}(0,x)
under the
L^{2}_{\rho_{0}}
-norm (see Lemma
A.1
). Second, let the corresponding Galerkin approximate solutions of the problem (
2.7
) have the forms
Y^{n,\delta}(t,x)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}^{n,\delta}(t)e_{j}(x)
with
\lambda_{j}^{n,\delta}(0)=\langle\ddot{U}_{0}^{\delta},e_{j}\rangle
. By the same diagonal density argument used after (
3.16
), the pairs
(n,\delta)
can be selected with
n,\delta^{-1}\to\infty
so that

|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}Y^{n,\delta}(0)|_{2}\leq C|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}U_{tt}(0)|_{2},

where
C>0
is independent of the pairs in this diagonal sequence. We then establish the desired uniform estimates for
Y^{n,\delta}
in
L^{2}_{\rho_{0}}
and take the limit as
n,\delta^{-1}\to\infty
along the same diagonal sequence to obtain a weak solution
w^{(2)}
to (
2.7
).

On the other hand, for the theory in
{\rm ii)}
,
we notice that
(w^{(0)},w^{(1)},w^{(2)})
satisfy the equations in (
3.2
), (
2.6
), (
2.7
) in the sense of distributions, respectively, i.e., for all
\varphi\in H^{1}_{\rho_{0}}
,

(2.8)

\big<\rho_{0}w^{(i)}_{t},\varphi\big>_{H^{-1}_{\rho_{0}}\times H^{1}_{\rho_{0}}}+\Big<\frac{\rho_{0}w^{(i)}_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}},\varphi_{x}\Big>=\big<\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\bar{R}^{(i)},\varphi_{x}\big>,\quad i=0,1,2,

where
\bar{R}^{(i)}
(
i=0,1,2
) denote some remainders. Denoting

Y^{(1)}=\int_{0}^{t}w^{(1)}\,\mathrm{d}s+u_{0}-U\ \ \text{and}\ \ Y^{(2)}=\int_{0}^{t}w^{(2)}\,\mathrm{d}s+U_{t}(0,x)-U_{t},

integrating (
2.8
) over
[0,t]
for
i=1
and
2
, and then subtracting the resulting equations from (
2.8
) for
i=0
and
1
, respectively, one has that
(Y^{(1)},Y^{(2)})
satisfy

(2.9)

\big<\rho_{0}Y^{(i)}_{t},\varphi\big>_{H^{-1}_{\rho_{0}}\times H^{1}_{\rho_{0}}}+\Big<\frac{\rho_{0}Y^{(i)}_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}},\varphi_{x}\Big>=\big<R^{(i)}_{1},\varphi_{x}\big>+\big<R^{(i)}_{2},\varphi\big>,\quad i=1,2,

where
(R^{(i)}_{1},R^{(i)}_{2})
(
i=1,2
) denote some remainders. By choosing
\varphi=Y^{(i)}
, one gets from the energy estimates that
Y^{(i)}=0
, and thus
(w^{(1)},w^{(2)})=(U_{t},U_{tt})
a.e.

2.2.3.
A new cross-derivatives embedding theorem

The degeneracy of the spatial dissipation
near the moving boundary makes it hard to establish the desired elliptic estimates. In order to overcome this difficulty, motivated by the energy functional that we choose, we provide a novel cross-derivatives embedding theorem (see Proposition
C.1
in Appendix
C
). Specifically, it is shown that under some carefully chosen parameter
s
,

(2.10)

\underbrace{|\rho_{0}^{s}\partial_{x}^{j+1}U|_{2}}_{\text{high order term}}\leq C(s)\big(\underbrace{|\rho_{0}^{s}\partial_{x}^{j+1}U+\kappa\rho_{0}^{s-1}(\rho_{0})_{x}\partial_{x}^{j}U|_{2}}_{\text{crossing term}}+\underbrace{|\rho_{0}^{s}\partial_{x}^{j}U|_{2}}_{\text{lower order term}}\!\!\big)\ \ \text{for }j\in\mathbb{N},

which means that the weighted
L^{2}
estimate on the higher order spatial derivatives of
U
can be dominated by the
L^{2}
estimate on the crossing term and the weighted
L^{2}
estimate on the lower order spatial derivatives of
U
while keeping weights unchanged.
It should be pointed out that for the special
\alpha=1
,
the original version of (
2.10
), i.e.,

(2.11)

\big|\rho_{0}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{x}^{j+1}U\big|_{2}\leq C(s)\Big(\big|\rho_{0}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{x}^{j+1}U+\kappa\rho_{0}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\rho_{0})_{x}\partial_{x}^{j}U\big|_{2}+|\rho_{0}^{s}\partial_{x}^{j}U|_{2}\Big)\ \ \text{for }j\in\mathbb{N},

which was first announced in
[
12
,
13
]
, was applied to establish the elliptic estimates for
U
in
[
35
,
36
]
. Compared with (
2.10
), (
2.11
) leads to the increasing of the power of weights when establishing the higher order elliptic estimate from the lower order one. Consequently, if one starts with
\sqrt{\rho_{0}}U_{x}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2})
, it follows from (
2.11
) that the optimal elliptic estimates are
\rho_{0}^{\frac{j}{2}}\partial_{x}^{j}U\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2})
for
j\geq 2
, and thus it is required that
\rho_{0}\in H^{5}
and
u_{0}
stays in one weighted
H^{6}
space in
[
35
]
. In the present paper, Proposition
C.1
allows us to reduce the initial regularity required for local existence and uniqueness of classical solutions to
\rho_{0}\in H^{3}
and
u_{0}
belonging to a weighted
H^{4}
space.

2.2.4.
Some new global weighted estimates for the effective velocity independent of the BD entropy

As observed in
\eqref{lagrange}_{2}
or
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{1}
, in order to establish the desired global-in-time energy estimates for
U
, the global-in-time lower and upper bounds of
\eta_{x}
, and the regularity estimates of
H_{x}
or
\eta_{x}
are required. For the Cauchy problem of the 1-D shallow water equations with far field vacuum, when the initial BD entropy condition (
1.9
) holds, in
[
8
]
, one can use the classical BD entropy estimate, i.e.,
\|\sqrt{\rho}(u+(\log\rho)_{y})(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}<\infty
for arbitrarily large time
t
, to provide the global-in-time upper bound of the effective velocity
v=u+(\log\rho)_{y}
in Eulerian coordinates. Then the derivative
\rho_{y}
in the momentum equations can be controlled by the quantity
\rho(v-u)
. However, when
\alpha=1
in (
1.8
), (
1.9
) fails, so this type of argument cannot be applied to our analysis.
In order to overcome this difficulty, we developed some new weighted
L^{p}
-estimates (
2\leq p\leq\infty
) of
V
, especially the weighted
L^{\infty}
-estimate of
V
, which do not require the initial BD entropy condition (
1.9
) or the physical vacuum boundary condition (
1.10
), and play a crucial role in establishing the global upper bound of
\eta_{x}
and the uniform estimates for
U
.

On the one hand, it follows from the time evolution equation (
6.6
) of
V
that

(2.12)

V(t,x)=e^{-\int_{0}^{t}2H\,\mathrm{d}s}V(0,x)+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{2\rho_{0}U}{\eta_{x}}e^{-\int_{\tau}^{t}2H\,\mathrm{d}s}\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

Then multiplying above by
\rho_{0}^{K}
for some
K>0
, one can obtain that for
2\leq p<\infty
,

(2.13)

\displaystyle|\rho_{0}^{K}V(t)|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq|\rho_{0}^{K}V(0)|_{p}+2\int_{0}^{t}\Big|\frac{\rho_{0}^{1+K}U}{\eta_{x}}\Big|_{p}\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

Actually, the boundedness of
|\rho_{0}^{K}V(t)|_{p}
is basically determined by that of
|\rho_{0}^{K}V(0)|_{p}
, since the global-in-time weighted
L^{p}
-estimates of
U
in (
2.13
) are somewhat easy to derive.
Consequently, we can choose suitable
K
in (
2.13
) to get the desired weighted
L^{p}

(2\leq p<\infty)
estimates of
V
in Lemma
6.5
, i.e.,

(2.14)

\rho_{0}^{r\alpha}V\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{p})\quad\text{for}\quad r>\frac{p-1}{p},\,\,2\leq p<\infty.

On the other hand, we find that the Sobolev embedding theorem and (
2.14
) give

(2.15)

\int_{0}^{t}\Big|\frac{\rho_{0}^{1+\alpha}U}{\eta_{x}}\Big|_{\infty}\,\mathrm{d}s\ <\infty\ \ \text{for all }0\leq t\leq T,

which, along with (
2.12
), yields that (see Lemma
6.6
)

(2.16)

\rho_{0}^{\alpha}V\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty}).

2.2.5.
Global-in-time lower and upper bounds of
\eta_{x}

Now we show how to derive the uniform lower and upper bounds for
\eta_{x}
when there is no restriction on the size of the initial data, particularly for the case
\alpha=1
, where the classical BD entropy estimates fail.

The uniform lower bound of
\eta_{x}
is motivated from the following several observations:

•

we can get the global uniform upper bound of
H

only by the fundamental energy estimates (
6.2
) (see Lemma
6.2
);

•

The
L^{q}
(
2\leq q<\infty
) energy estimates of
U
in Lemma
6.3
imply a refined upper bound of
H
, that is,
H\leq C(T)\rho_{0}
.

•

(
1.15
) and the refined global upper bound of
H
imply

(2.17)

H(t,x)=\frac{\rho_{0}(x)}{\eta_{x}(t,x)}\leq C(T)\rho_{0}\implies\eta_{x}(t,x)\geq C(T)^{-1}.

To establish the global-in-time upper bound of
\eta_{x}
, we first use
the weighted logarithmic estimate (
2.18
) to control the interior
region.

On the other hand, for the global upper bound of
\eta_{x}
, our first aim is to establish

(2.18)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\rho_{0}^{K}\log\eta_{x}|_{\infty}\leq C(K,T),

for some
K>0
(see Lemma
6.11
), which is motivated by the identity

(2.19)

\log\eta_{x}(t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Therefore, we only need to show that

(2.20)

\rho_{0}^{K}\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}}\in L^{1}([0,T];L^{\infty}).

Actually, (
2.18
) only ensures that
\eta_{x}\leq C(\sigma,T)
in
[0,T]\times[\sigma,1-\sigma]
for any
\sigma\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. To get the upper bound of
\eta_{x}
near the vacuum boundary, we analyze equation
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{1}
and derive the additional
\rho_{0}
-decay of the boundary momentum integral, thereby obtaining
H\geq C(T)^{-1}\rho_{0}
in a neighborhood whose width
depends only on the initial data, the fixed parameters, and
T
.
Combining the interior and boundary estimates, together with the boundary condition
\eta_{x}|_{x\in\Gamma}=1
, gives the desired
upper bound of
\eta_{x}
.

Turning back to the derivation of (
2.20
), we develop an unconventional technique:
some
\eta_{x}
-weighted energy estimates for
(U,U_{x})
.
For convenience, let
K>0
be a generic parameter. Formally, to get (
2.20
), one needs to derive some weighted
L^{2}
-estimate of
(\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}})_{x}
by the Sobolev embedding
H^{1}\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}
. If we just multiply
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{1}
by
\rho_{0}^{K}U
and
\rho_{0}^{K}U_{t}
successively, and then integrate these resulting equalities, we shall obtain

(2.21)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}^{K}U,\ \ \rho_{0}^{K}\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}),\quad\rho_{0}^{K}U_{t},\ \ \frac{\rho_{0}^{K}}{\eta_{x}}\Big(\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}}\Big)_{x}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}),

where the estimate for
\frac{\rho_{0}^{K}}{\eta_{x}}(\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}})_{x}
can be obtained from
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{1}
and that of
\rho_{0}^{K}U_{t}
. However, (
2.21
) cannot lead to (
2.20
), since there are ”too many
\eta_{x}
” appearing in the denominators of
\rho_{0}^{K}\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}}
and
\frac{\rho_{0}^{K}}{\eta_{x}}(\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}})_{x}
. To address this issue, we multiply
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{1}
by
\rho_{0}^{K}\eta_{x}U
and
\rho_{0}^{K}\eta_{x}U_{t}
(introducing the weight
\eta_{x}
), and then derive the following types of estimates:

(2.22)

\rho_{0}^{K}\sqrt{\eta_{x}}U,\ \rho_{0}^{K}\frac{U_{x}}{\sqrt{\eta_{x}}}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}),\quad\rho_{0}^{K}\sqrt{\eta_{x}}U_{t},\ \frac{\rho_{0}^{K}}{\sqrt{\eta_{x}}}\left(\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}}\right)_{x}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}).

The above estimates, together with Lemma
A.4
, would lead to (
2.20
), that is,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\Big|\rho_{0}^{K}\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}}\Big|_{\infty}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\sqrt{T}\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\Big|\rho_{0}^{K}\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}}\Big|_{\infty}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C(T)+\int_{0}^{T}\bigg|\left(\rho_{0}^{2K}\frac{U_{x}^{2}}{\eta_{x}^{2}}\right)_{x}\bigg|_{1}\mathrm{d}t

(2.23)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(T)+2\int_{0}^{T}\bigg|\rho_{0}^{2K}\Big|\frac{U_{x}}{\sqrt{\eta_{x}}}\Big|\Big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{\eta_{x}}}\left(\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}}\right)_{x}\Big|\bigg|_{1}\mathrm{d}t+(\text{Remainders})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(T)+\underbrace{2\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Big|\rho_{0}^{K}\frac{U_{x}}{\sqrt{\eta_{x}}}\Big|_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\Big|\frac{\rho_{0}^{K}}{\sqrt{\eta_{x}}}\left(\frac{U_{x}}{\eta_{x}}\right)_{x}\Big|_{2}\,\mathrm{d}t}_{\text{controlled by \eqref{edt}}}+(\text{Remainders})\leq C(T).

The detailed calculations of (
2.22
) and (
2.23
) can be found in Lemmas
6.9
-
6.10
and Corollary
6.2
, respectively.

2.2.6.
Global weighted energy estimates

For establishing the global weighted energy estimates, first, using (
6.1
), we rewrite
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{1}
as follows:

\rho_{0}U_{t}-\left(\frac{\rho_{0}U_{x}}{\eta_{x}^{2}}\right)_{x}+2\frac{\rho_{0}^{2}}{\eta_{x}}(V-U)=0,

which, along with the global lower and upper bounds of
\eta_{x}
, and the new weighted
L^{p}
estimate for
V
,
yields all the following tangential estimates (see §
7.1
)

(2.24)

\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\partial_{t}^{j}U,\,\,\sqrt{\rho_{0}}\partial_{t}^{k}U_{x}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2})\ \ \text{for }j=0,1,2\text{ and }k=0,1.

Next, for the elliptic estimates, we take
\rho_{0}^{\left(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}\right)\alpha}\partial_{x}^{3}U
, for example, and the other ones can be obtained similarly. In §
7.2
, according to (
2.14
), (
2.16
), the lower and upper bounds of
\eta_{x}
, and (
2.24
), we first obtain

\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}\left(\eta_{x}^{-1}U_{x}\right)_{xx}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2})
for any
\varepsilon>0
from
\eqref{secondreformulation}_{1}
.
Second, applying
\partial_{x}^{2}
to (
2.19
) leads to the following identity:

\partial_{x}^{3}\eta=\eta_{x}^{-1}\eta_{xx}^{2}+\eta_{x}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\eta_{x}^{-1}U_{x}\right)_{xx}\,\mathrm{d}s,

It follows that

\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}\partial_{x}^{3}\eta\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2})
.
Thus
\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}\partial_{x}^{3}U\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2})
, which, by carefully choosing
\varepsilon
and applying Proposition
C.1
, yields
\rho_{0}^{\left(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}\right)\alpha}\partial_{x}^{3}U\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2})
.

3.
Global existence and uniqueness for the linearized problems

We will give the proofs for the local existence and uniqueness results stated in Theorem
1.1
in §3-§5. In this section, we first linearize the reformulated problems (
2.1
)-(
2.2
), and then establish the global existence and uniqueness of classical solutions to the linearized problems by the Galerkin scheme.
For convenience, denote by
\left<\cdot,\cdot\right>_{X^{*}\times X}
the pairing between the space
X
and its dual space
X^{*}
, and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
the inner product of
L^{2}
, that is,

\left<F,f\right>_{X^{*}\times X}:=F(f)\ \ \text{for }F\in X^{*},f\in X;\quad\langle f,g\rangle:=\int fg\,\mathrm{d}x\ \ \text{for }f,g\in L^{2}.

In particular, if
X\hookrightarrow L^{2}\hookrightarrow X^{*}
, and
F\in L^{2}
, then
\left<F,f\right>_{X^{*}\times X}=\langle F,f\rangle
. In addition, we denote by
\left<F,f\right>_{X_{t}^{*}(Y^{*})\times X_{t}(Y)}
the pairing between the vector-valued space
X([0,T];Y)
and its dual space
X^{*}([0,T];Y^{*})
.

3.1.
Linearization

In order to solve the nonlinear problems (
2.1
)-(
2.2
), one needs to consider the following linearized ones:

•

when
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{3}
,

(3.1)

\begin{cases}\quad\displaystyle\phi_{0}^{2}U_{t}-\left(\frac{\phi_{0}^{2}U_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}\right)_{x}\\[8.0pt]
\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha}-2\Big)\frac{\phi_{0}(\phi_{0})_{x}U_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}-\bigg(\frac{\phi_{0}^{2+\frac{1}{\alpha}}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}\bigg)_{x}+\Big(2-\frac{1}{\alpha}\Big)\frac{\phi_{0}^{1+\frac{1}{\alpha}}(\phi_{0})_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}&\text{in }(0,T]\times I,\\[8.0pt]
\quad U=u_{0}&\text{on }\{t=0\}\times I;\end{cases}

•

when
\frac{1}{3}<\alpha\leq 1
,

(3.2)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\phi_{0}^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}U_{t}-\bigg(\frac{\phi_{0}^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}U_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}\bigg)_{x}+\bigg(\frac{\phi_{0}^{\frac{2}{\alpha}}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}\bigg)_{x}=0&\text{in }(0,T]\times I,\\[8.0pt]
U=u_{0}&\text{on }\{t=0\}\times I,\end{cases}

where
\bar{\eta}
stands for the flow map corresponding to
\bar{U}
,

(3.3)

\bar{\eta}(t,x)=x+\int_{0}^{t}\bar{U}(s,x)\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad\bar{\eta}|_{t=0}=\mathrm{id},

and
\bar{U}
is a given function satisfying that
\bar{U}(0,x)=u_{0}(x)
for
x\in I
, and for any
T>0
,

•

if
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{3}
,

\bar{U}\in C([0,T];H^{3})\cap C^{1}([0,T];H^{1}),\ \ \sup_{t\in[0,T]}E(t,\bar{U})<\infty,\ \ \bar{U}\in\mathscr{C}([0,T];E);

•

if
\frac{1}{3}<\alpha\leq 1
,

\ \ \ \ \bar{U}\in C([0,T];W^{3,1})\cap C^{1}([0,T];W^{1,1}),\ \ \sup_{t\in[0,T]}\widetilde{E}(t,\bar{U})<\infty,\ \ \bar{U}\in\mathscr{C}([0,T];\widetilde{E}).

Moreover, it will be assumed here that there exists a time
\widetilde{T}>0
such that
\frac{1}{2}\leq\bar{\eta}_{x}\leq\frac{3}{2}
on
[0,\widetilde{T}]\times\bar{I}
, which will be shown in §
4.1
-§
4.2
for our linearization procedure, and we assume also
T\in(0,\widetilde{T}]
. The main result in this section on the global existence and uniqueness for the linear problems above can be stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose that (
2.3
) and (
1.19
) or (
1.24
) hold. Then for any
0<T\leq\widetilde{T}
,

\mathrm{i)}

if
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{3}
, (
3.1
) admits a unique classical solution
U
in
[0,T]\times\bar{I}
satisfying

U\in C([0,T];H^{3})\cap C^{1}([0,T];H^{1}),\ \ \sup_{t\in[0,T]}E(t,U)<\infty,\ \ U\in\mathscr{C}([0,T];E);

\mathrm{ii)}

if
\frac{1}{3}<\alpha\leq 1
, (
3.2
) admits a unique classical solution
U
in
[0,T]\times\bar{I}
satisfying

U\in C([0,T];W^{3,1})\cap C^{1}([0,T];W^{1,1}),\ \ \sup_{t\in[0,T]}\widetilde{E}(t,U)<\infty,\ \ U\in\mathscr{C}([0,T];\widetilde{E}).

Moreover,
U
satisfies the Neumann boundary condition,
U_{x}(t,x)=0
on
[0,T]\times\Gamma
.

3.2.
The Galerkin method: weak solutions to some general degenerate systems

Before proving Lemma
3.1
, we first show the global-in-time existence of weak solutions to the general problems (
3.4
) and (
3.31
) via the Galerkin method (see Propositions
3.1
-
3.2
). These results will be extensively used in §
3.3
-§
3.4
for the proof of Lemma
3.1
.

3.2.1.
Case
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{3}

In the first case
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{3}
, we consider the following initial boundary value problem:

(3.4)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\phi_{0}^{2}w_{t}-\left(\frac{\phi_{0}^{2}w_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}\right)_{x}=K_{0}\frac{\phi_{0}(\phi_{0})_{x}w_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}-\left(\phi_{0}P_{1}\right)_{x}+P_{2}&\text{in }(0,T]\times I,\\
w=w_{0}&\text{on }\{t=0\}\times I,\end{cases}

where
P_{1},P_{2}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2})
,
w_{0}\in L^{2}_{\phi_{0}^{2}}
and
K_{0}\geq 0
is a given constant, and the definition of weak solutions of this problem can be given as follows.

Definition 3.1
.

A function
w(t,x)
is said to be a weak solution in
[0,T]\times I
to (
3.4
), if

\mathrm{i)}

\phi_{0}w\in C([0,T];L^{2}),\quad\phi_{0}w_{x}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}),\quad\phi_{0}^{2}w_{t}\in L^{2}([0,T];H^{-1}_{\phi_{0}^{2}})
;

\mathrm{ii)}

the following equation holds for all
\varphi\in H^{1}_{\phi_{0}^{2}}
and a.e. time
0<t\leq T
,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\qquad\left<\phi_{0}^{2}w_{t},\varphi\right>_{H^{-1}_{\phi_{0}^{2}}\times H^{1}_{\phi_{0}^{2}}}\!+\left<\frac{\phi_{0}^{2}w_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}},\varphi_{x}\right>

\displaystyle=\left<K_{0}\frac{\phi_{0}(\phi_{0})_{x}w_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}+P_{2},\varphi\right>+\left<\phi_{0}P_{1},\varphi_{x}\right>;

\mathrm{iii)}

w(0,x)=w_{0}(x)
for a.e.
x\in I
.

The aim of §
3.2.1
is to establish the following existence theory and the related estimates.

Proposition 3.1
.

For all
0<T\leq\widetilde{T}
, there exists a unique weak solution
w
in
[0,T]\times I
to the problem (
3.4
), satisfying the following estimate:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\phi_{0}w\right|_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\big(\left|\phi_{0}w_{x}\right|_{2}^{2}+\|\phi_{0}^{2}w_{t}\|_{-1,\phi_{0}^{2}}^{2}\,\big)\mathrm{d}t\leq C\Big(\left|\phi_{0}w_{0}\right|_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}(\left|P_{1}\right|_{2}^{2}+\left|P_{2}\right|_{2}^{2}\,)\mathrm{d}t\Big).

Proof.

Step 1: introduction of the Galerkin scheme.
First, it follows from Lemma
A.1
that, for given
w_{0}\in L^{2}_{\phi_{0}^{2}}
, there exists a smooth sequence
\{w_{0}^{\delta}\}_{\delta>0}\subset C^{\infty}(\bar{I})
satisfying

(3.6)

\phi_{0}w^{\delta}_{0}\to\phi_{0}w_{0}\ \ \text{in }L^{2}\quad\text{as}\quad\delta\rightarrow 0.

Second, by solving the eigenvalue problem
-\Delta e+e=\lambda e
with the Neumann boundary condition (see Chapter 9 of
[
53
]
), one can choose a Hilbert basis
\{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
of
H^{1}
with
e_{j}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{I})
and
(e_{j})_{x}|_{x\in\Gamma}=0
(
j\geq 1
), which is orthonormal in
L^{2}
and orthogonal in
H^{1}
.

Next, given any
0<\delta<1
and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, set

(3.7)

X^{n,\delta}(t,x):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mu_{k}^{n,\delta}(t)e_{k}(x),

where
\mu_{k}^{n,\delta}(t)
are selected by solving the following initial value problem of the ODE system:

(3.8)

\begin{cases}\quad\displaystyle\big<\phi_{0}^{2}X^{n,\delta}_{t},e_{j}\big>+\bigg<\frac{\phi_{0}^{2}X^{n,\delta}_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}},(e_{j})_{x}\bigg>\\[10.0pt]
\displaystyle=K_{0}\bigg<\frac{\phi_{0}(\phi_{0})_{x}X^{n,\delta}_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}},e_{j}\bigg>+\left<\phi_{0}P_{1},(e_{j})_{x}\right>+\left<P_{2},e_{j}\right>\ \ \text{in }(0,T],\\[10.0pt]
\quad\mu_{j}^{n,\delta}(0)=\left<w_{0}^{\delta},e_{j}\right>,\,\,j=1,2,\cdots,n.\end{cases}

For simplicity, one can rewrite (
3.8
) as

(3.9)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\mathbb{A}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mu^{n,\delta}(t)+\mathbb{B}(t)\cdot\mu^{n,\delta}(t)=\mathbb{C}(t)\ \ \text{in }(0,T],\\[10.0pt]
\mu_{j}^{n,\delta}(0)=\left<w_{0}^{\delta},e_{j}\right>,\,\,j=1,2,\cdots,n,\end{cases}

where

\begin{split}\mu^{n,\delta}(t):=&(\mu_{j}^{n,\delta}(t))_{j=1}^{n},\quad\mathbb{A}=\left(\int\phi_{0}^{2}e_{k}e_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)_{k,j=1}^{n},\\
\mathbb{B}(t)=&\left(\int\frac{\phi_{0}^{2}(e_{k})_{x}(e_{j})_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x-K_{0}\int\frac{\phi_{0}(\phi_{0})_{x}(e_{k})_{x}e_{j}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)_{k,j=1}^{n},\\
\mathbb{C}(t)=&\left(\int\phi_{0}P_{1}(e_{j})_{x}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int P_{2}e_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)_{j=1}^{n}.\end{split}

In order to solve (
3.9
), one needs first to show that the matrix
\mathbb{A}
is non-singular.

Lemma 3.2
.

The vectors
\{\phi_{0}e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, are linearly independent. In particular, the matrix
\mathbb{A}
is non-singular for each
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.

Proof.

Since
\{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
are linearly independent, so are
\{\phi_{0}e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
.
Hence its Gram matrix
\mathbb{A}
is, of course, non-singular.
∎

In addition, one can check that
\mathbb{B}(t)\in W^{2,\infty}(0,T)
,
\mathbb{C}(t)\in L^{2}(0,T)
. Thus, based on standard ODEs’ theory, one can show the following existence result.

Lemma 3.3
.

There exists a small time
0<T_{n}\leq\widetilde{T}
which depends only on
n
, such that (
3.9
) admits a unique solution
\mu_{j}^{n,\delta}\in AC[0,T_{n}]
, for each
j=1,2,\cdots,n
. Here,
AC
stands for the space of absolutely continuous functions. Consequently,
X^{n,\delta}(t,x)
belongs to
AC([0,T_{n}];C^{\infty}(\bar{I}))
, and
X^{n,\delta}
is differentiable a.e. in
t
, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
0<\delta<1
.

Proof.

Consider the general recursive integral equations:

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mu^{k+1}(t)

\displaystyle=\mu_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\big(\bar{\mathbb{C}}(\tau)-\bar{\mathbb{B}}(\tau)\mu^{k}(\tau)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\tau,\quad k\in\mathbb{N},

where
\mu^{0}(t)=\mu_{0}=(w_{0}^{\delta},e_{j})
,
\bar{\mathbb{B}}(t):=\mathbb{A}^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{B}(t)
,
\bar{\mathbb{C}}(t):=\mathbb{A}^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{C}(t)
.

Next, set
\hat{\mu}^{k}(t)=\mu^{k}(t)-\mu^{k-1}(t)
. It follows from (
3.10
) that

\hat{\mu}^{k+1}(t)=-\int_{0}^{t}\bar{\mathbb{B}}(\tau)\hat{\mu}^{k}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau,

which leads to

(3.11)

\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}|\hat{\mu}^{k+1}(\tau)|\leq t|\bar{\mathbb{B}}|_{\infty}\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}|\hat{\mu}^{k}(\tau)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}|\hat{\mu}^{k}(\tau)|,

provided that
t\leq T_{n}:=\min\{(2|\bar{\mathbb{B}}|_{\infty}+1)^{-1},\widetilde{T}\}
. Note that, by induction, (
3.11
) implies that
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T_{n}]}|\hat{\mu}^{k}(t)|<\infty
, and hence
\{\mu^{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
is a Cauchy sequence that converges uniformly to some limit
\mu\in L^{\infty}(0,T_{n})
, namely,

\mu^{k}\to\mu\ \ \text{in }L^{\infty}\ \ \text{as }k\to\infty.

Passing to the limit
k\to\infty
in (
3.10
) shows that

(3.12)

\mu(t)=\mu_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\bar{\mathbb{C}}(s)-\bar{\mathbb{B}}(s)\mu(s)\right)\,\mathrm{d}s\ \ \text{for all }0\leq t\leq T_{n},

which yields that
\mu(t)\in AC[0,T_{n}]
and
\eqref{mu^n}_{1}
holds for a.e.
t\in(0,T_{n})
. The uniqueness and continuity are direct consequences of (
3.12
). The proof of Lemma
3.3
is completed.
∎

Step 2: Uniform estimates of
X^{n,\delta}
.
First, multiplying both sides of (
3.8
) by
\mu^{n,\delta}_{j}(t)
, and then summing
j
from
1
to
n
, according to Lemma
A.5
, one obtains that

(3.13)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int\phi_{0}^{2}|X^{n,\delta}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int\frac{\phi_{0}^{2}|X^{n,\delta}_{x}|^{2}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underline{K_{0}\int\frac{\phi_{0}(\phi_{0})_{x}X^{n,\delta}_{x}X^{n,\delta}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x}_{:=I_{1}}+\int\phi_{0}P_{1}X^{n,\delta}_{x}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int P_{2}X^{n,\delta}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle I_{1}+C\big(\left|P_{1}\right|_{2}^{2}+\left|P_{2}\right|_{2}^{2}+|X^{n,\delta}|_{2}^{2}\big)+\frac{1}{8}|\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}_{x}|_{2}^{2}.

For
I_{1}
, it follows from integration by parts and Lemma
A.3
that

(3.14)

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\underline{-\frac{K_{0}}{2}\int\frac{((\phi_{0})_{x})^{2}|X^{n,\delta}|^{2}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x}_{\leq 0}-\frac{K_{0}}{2}\int\left(\frac{(\phi_{0})_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}}\right)_{x}\phi_{0}|X^{n,\delta}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C(K_{0})\left(\left|(\phi_{0})_{xx}\right|_{\infty}+\left|(\phi_{0})_{x}\right|_{\infty}\left|\bar{\eta}_{xx}\right|_{\infty}\right)\big|\phi_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}X^{n,\delta}\big|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(K_{0})|\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{8}|\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}_{x}|_{2}^{2},

which, along with (
3.13
) and Grönwall’s inequality, yields that

(3.15)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T_{n}]}|\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}|_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T_{n}}|\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}_{x}|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(K_{0})\Big(|\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}(0)|_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T_{n}}\big(\left|P_{1}\right|_{2}^{2}+\left|P_{2}\right|_{2}^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\Big),

where
X^{n,\delta}(0,x)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{j}^{n,\delta}(0)e_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big<w_{0}^{\delta},e_{j}\big>e_{j}
.

To get the uniform estimate of
\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}(0,x)
, on the one hand, it follows from
w_{0}^{\delta}\in L^{2}
and Lemma
A.6
(in Appendix
A
) that

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big<w_{0}^{\delta},e_{j}\big>e_{j}\to w_{0}^{\delta}\ \ \text{in }L^{2}\ \ \text{for any fixed }\delta>0\quad\text{as}\quad n\rightarrow\infty,

which yields that

(3.16)

\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}(0,x)\to\phi_{0}w_{0}^{\delta}\ \ \text{in }L^{2}\quad\text{as}\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

We now combine (
3.6
) and (
3.16
) by a
diagonal argument. If
w_{0}=0
, we choose
w_{0}^{\delta}=0
for
every
\delta>0
. We first choose

\delta
by (
3.6
) and then, with this
\delta
fixed,
choose
n
sufficiently large by (
3.16
). Thus, we can select a diagonal sequence of pairs
(n_{j},\delta_{j})
such that
n_{j},\delta_{j}^{-1}\to\infty
and

(3.17)

|\phi_{0}X^{n_{j},\delta_{j}}(0)-\phi_{0}w_{0}|_{2}\leq|\phi_{0}X^{n_{j},\delta_{j}}(0)-\phi_{0}w_{0}^{\delta_{j}}|_{2}+|\phi_{0}w_{0}^{\delta_{j}}-\phi_{0}w_{0}|_{2}\to 0.

In the rest of the proof, for brevity, all occurrences of
(X^{n,\delta},\mu^{n,\delta})
refer to this fixed diagonal sequence
(X^{n_{j},\delta_{j}},\mu^{n_{j},\delta_{j}})
,
and every limit as
n,\delta^{-1}\to\infty
is taken along
this sequence. If
w_{0}=0
, then
X^{n,\delta}(0)=0
; if
w_{0}\neq 0
, we discard finitely many pairs
if necessary. Hence, in both cases, we can obtain from (
3.17
) that

(3.18)

|\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}(0)|_{2}\leq 2|\phi_{0}w_{0}|_{2}.

Combining (
3.15
) and (
3.18
) along this diagonal sequence, one has for all
0\leq t\leq T_{n}
,

(3.19)

\displaystyle|\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}(t)|_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}|\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}_{x}|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C(K_{0})\Big(\left|\phi_{0}w_{0}\right|_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{\widetilde{T}}\big(\left|P_{1}\right|_{2}^{2}+\left|P_{2}\right|_{2}^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\Big).

For each
X^{n,\delta}
in the fixed diagonal sequence, it follows from (
3.19
) that the local solution
\mu^{n,\delta}
on
[0,T_{n}]
in Lemma
3.3
can be extended to a global one on
[0,T]
, for all
0<T\leq\widetilde{T}
. More precisely, assuming contrarily that
T_{n}<\widetilde{T}
is the maximal life span of
\mu^{n,\delta}
, according to (
3.19
), one has

(3.20)

|\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}(T_{n})|_{2}\leq\limsup_{t\to T_{n}^{-}}|\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}(t)|_{2}\leq C\implies X^{n,\delta}(T_{n},x)\in L^{2}_{\phi_{0}^{2}}.

Then
\mu_{j}^{n,\delta}(T_{n})=\left<X^{n,\delta}(T_{n}),e_{j}\right>
can be regarded as a new initial value of (
3.9
). Thanks to Lemma
3.3
, there exists a small time
T_{n}^{\prime}>0
, such that
\mu_{j}^{n,\delta}
exists uniquely on
[0,T_{n}+T_{n}^{\prime}]
, which contradicts our assumption. Therefore, (
3.19
) holds for any
0<T\leq\widetilde{T}
, that is,

(3.21)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}|_{2}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}|\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}_{x}|_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C(K_{0})\Big(\left|\phi_{0}w_{0}\right|_{2}^{2}+\!\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\big(\left|P_{1}\right|_{2}^{2}+\left|P_{2}\right|_{2}^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\Big).

Step 3: Taking the limit along the diagonal
sequence as
n,\delta^{-1}\to\infty
.
Based on (
3.21
), by the weak convergence argument,
one may extract a subsequence of the fixed diagonal sequence, still denoted by
X^{n,\delta}
, satisfying

(3.22)

\displaystyle\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}\rightharpoonup X_{1}

\displaystyle\text{weakly* in }L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}),

\displaystyle\phi_{0}X^{n,\delta}_{x}\rightharpoonup X_{2}

\displaystyle\text{weakly in }L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}),

for some limits
X_{1},X_{2}
. By Lemma
A.5
, it holds that
\{X^{n,\delta}\}\subset L^{2}([0,T];L^{2})
and

X^{n,\delta}\rightharpoonup w\quad\text{weakly in }L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}),

which, by the definition of weak derivatives, yields that

X_{1}=\phi_{0}w
and
X_{2}=\phi_{0}w_{x}
.
Moreover, the above weak convergences imply that (
3.21
) also holds for
w
.

We now pass to the limit as
n,\delta^{-1}\to\infty
along this subsequence in (
3.8
). Setting
\Phi^{m}(t,x)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\xi_{j}(t)e_{j}
, where
\xi(t)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,T)
, we note that, for each fixed
m
, the condition
n\geq m
holds eventually along this
subsequence. It then follows from (
3.8
) that

(3.23)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\big<\phi_{0}^{2}X^{n,\delta}_{t},\Phi^{m}\big>\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\bigg<\frac{\phi_{0}^{2}X^{n,\delta}_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}},\Phi_{x}^{m}\bigg>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{0}\int_{0}^{T}\bigg<\frac{\phi_{0}(\phi_{0})_{x}X^{n,\delta}_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}},\Phi^{m}\bigg>\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\left<\phi_{0}P_{1},\Phi_{x}^{m}\right>\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\left<P_{2},\Phi^{m}\right>\,\mathrm{d}t.

Next, since
X^{n,\delta}
and
\Phi^{m}
are regular with respect to
t
, then in (
3.23
), one can transfer
\partial_{t}
from
X^{n,\delta}
to
\Phi^{m}
, and then let
n,\delta^{-1}\to\infty
along this subsequence, which, combined with (
3.22
), yields

(3.24)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\left<\phi_{0}^{2}w,\Phi^{m}_{t}\right>\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\left<\frac{\phi_{0}^{2}w_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}},\Phi_{x}^{m}\right>\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{0}\int_{0}^{T}\left<\frac{\phi_{0}(\phi_{0})_{x}w_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}},\Phi^{m}\right>\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\left<\phi_{0}P_{1},\Phi_{x}^{m}\right>\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\left<P_{2},\Phi^{m}\right>\,\mathrm{d}t.

Since
\phi_{0}^{2}w\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2})
,
\phi_{0}^{2}w_{t}\in H^{-1}([0,T];L^{2})\subset H^{-1}([0,T];(H^{1})^{*})
, it follows from (
3.24
),
Lemma
A.5
and the definition of distributional derivatives that

\displaystyle\big|\big<\phi_{0}^{2}w_{t},\Phi^{m}\big>_{H_{t}^{-1}((H^{1})^{*})\times H^{1}_{0,t}(H^{1})}\big|=\Big|\int_{0}^{T}\left<\phi_{0}^{2}w,\Phi^{m}_{t}\right>\,\mathrm{d}t\Big|

(3.25)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\Big|\left<\frac{\phi_{0}^{2}w_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}},\Phi_{x}^{m}\right>\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t+K_{0}\int_{0}^{T}\Big|\left<\frac{\phi_{0}(\phi_{0})_{x}w_{x}}{\bar{\eta}_{x}^{2}},\Phi^{m}\right>\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\left|\left<\phi_{0}P_{1},\Phi_{x}^{m}\right>\right|\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\left|\left<P_{2},\Phi^{m}\right>\right|\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(K_{0})\Big(\left\|\phi_{0}w_{x}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2})}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left\|P_{i}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2})}\Big)\left\|\Phi^{m}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t}(H^{1}_{\phi_{0}^{2}})},

for which we can use Lemma
A.8
to extend
\phi_{0}^{2}w_{t}
from a functional defined on
H^{1}_{0}([0,T];H^{1})
to a functional defined on
L^{2}([0,T];H^{1}_{\phi_{0}^{2}})
, and obtain that

(3.26)

\|\phi_{0}^{2}w_{t}\|_{L^{2}_{t}(H^{-1}_{\phi_{0}^{2}})}\leq C(K_{0})\Big(\left\|\phi_{0}w_{x}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2})}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left\|P_{i}\right\|_{L^{2}_{t}(L^{2})}\Big)\leq C(K_{0}).

Here, according to the conditions of Lemma
A.8
, one still has to check that
\left\{\Phi^{m}\right\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is dense in
L^{2}([0,T];H^{1}_{\phi_{0}^{2}})
to ensure the uniqueness of the extension. Indeed, it suffices to prove the density of
\mathrm{span}\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
in
H^{1}_{\phi_{0}^{2}}
. First, one deduces from Lemma
A.1
that, for any given
f\in H^{1}_{\phi_{0}^{2}}
, there exists a sequence
\{f^{\delta}\}_{\delta>0}\subset C^{\infty}(\bar{I})
satisfying

f^{\delta}\to f\ \ \text{in }H^{1}_{\phi_{0}^{2}}\ \ \text{as }\delta\to 0.

Next, for every
g\in H^{1}
, according to Lemma
A.6
,

\sum_{j=1}^{m}\langle g,e_{j}\rangle e_{j}
converges to
g
in
H^{1}
, and hence in
H^{1}_{\phi_{0}^{2}}
as
m\to\infty
. Setting
f^{m,\delta}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\langle f^{\delta},e_{j}\rangle e_{j}
